\|\Lambda_{u}(n,\omega)\pi_{u}x\|\geq 1/(2K_{\epsilon}(\omega))

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
when
\|\pi_{u}x\|>1/2
since
\lambda_{\tau}-2\epsilon>0
. Then, (
10.11
) is true by observing two facts that
F(\omega,x)=\Lambda(\omega)x
for all
x\notin V(\omega)
, whose diameter is
\rho(\omega)<1/(2K_{\epsilon}(\omega))
(by (
3.6
)),
and that the stable foliation is unique.

Since
{\mathcal{W}}_{s}(\omega,x)
is the graph of the
mapping

y_{s}\mapsto\pi_{u}\{x+q_{0}(\omega,x,y_{s})\},

as mentioned in (
10.1
), we obtain from (
10.2
) and (
10.11
) that

\displaystyle\quad\pi_{u}q_{0}(\omega,x,y_{s})=0\quad{\rm if}~\pi_{u}x=0~{\rm or}~\|\pi_{u}x\|>1/2,\quad\forall y_{s}\in X_{s}.

(10.13)

Then, for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\pi_{u}z=0
or
\|\pi_{u}z\|>1/2
, we know from Lemma
10.1
that

\displaystyle\pi_{u}\phi^{-1}(\omega,z)

\displaystyle=\pi_{u}\{z+\pi_{s}p_{0}(\omega,\pi_{s}z,\pi_{u}\phi^{-1}(\omega,z))+\pi_{u}q_{0}(\omega,\pi_{u}z,\pi_{s}\phi^{-1}(\omega,z))\}

\displaystyle=\pi_{u}\{z+\pi_{s}p_{0}(\omega,\pi_{s}z,\pi_{u}\phi^{-1}(\omega,z))\}=\pi_{u}z.

(10.14)

In what follows, for any
x\in U_{1}\backslash X_{s}
, we want to find an integer
N\in\mathbb{N}
(depending on
\omega
and
x
) such that
\|\pi_{u}F(N,\omega,x)\|>1/2
, and then establish a relation between
\pi_{u}q_{0}(\omega,x,0)

and
\pi_{u}q_{0}(\theta^{N}\omega,F(N,\omega,x),0)
, which is equal to
0
by (
10.13
), to show the measurability of
\partial_{x}(\pi_{u}q_{0})(\omega,x,0)
(see (
10.29
) below). Basically, such an integer
N
exists since
the norm of the unstable projection
\pi_{u}F(n,\omega,x)
tends to infinity as
n\to\infty
for any
x\in U_{1}\backslash X_{s}
, but the integer
N
depends on
\omega
and we do not know its measurability. In order to solve this problem, after the following preparations, we will give our Lemma
10.2
.

Noting that
F_{s}(\omega,\cdot)
and

F_{u}(\omega,\cdot)
are tempered
C^{2,\alpha}
random diffeomorphisms such that
DF_{s}(\omega,{{0}})=\Lambda_{s}(\omega)
and
DF_{u}(\omega,{{0}})=\Lambda_{u}(\omega)
,
we obtain from
[
44
, Main Theorem]
that there are locally tempered
C^{1,\beta_{\alpha}}
(without loss of generality, we still use
\beta_{\alpha}
to denote the Hölder exponent here) random diffeomorphisms
\psi_{s}(\omega,\cdot):X_{s}\to X_{s}
and
\psi_{u}(\omega,\cdot):X_{u}\to X_{u}

such that
\psi_{s}(\omega,0)=\psi_{u}(\omega,0)=0
and

\displaystyle\psi_{s}\circ F_{s}\circ\psi_{s}^{-1}(\omega,x)=\Lambda_{s}(\omega),\qquad\psi_{u}\circ F_{u}\circ\psi_{u}^{-1}(\omega,x)=\Lambda_{u}(\omega).

(10.15)

Remark that although
\psi_{s}^{\pm 1}(\omega,\cdot)
and
\psi_{u}^{\pm 1}(\omega,\cdot)
are local mappings, a similar manner as (
9.14
) helps us extend them to global ones, which are tempered
C^{1,\beta_{\alpha}}
on any domain bounded by a tempered random variable.
Combining this fact with (
10.9
) and putting

\displaystyle\Phi(\omega,\cdot):=\psi(\omega,\cdot)\circ\phi(\omega,\cdot)\quad\mbox{for $\omega\in\Omega$}

(10.16)

with
\psi(\omega,x):=\psi_{s}(\omega,\pi_{s}x)+\psi_{u}(\omega,\pi_{u}x)
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\|\Phi(\omega,x)-\Phi(\omega,\tilde{x})\|\leq L_{3}(\omega)\|x-\tilde{x}\|^{\hat{\sigma}},\quad\forall x,\tilde{x}\in U_{1},

(10.17)

where
L_{3}(\omega)>0
is a random variable tempered from above.

For the estimate of
\Phi^{-1}(\omega,\cdot)
, we
set
y:=\Phi(\omega,x)
for
x\in U_{1}
and notice that

\displaystyle\|\psi_{u}^{-1}(\omega,\pi_{u}y)\|\leq\hat{L}_{\epsilon}(\omega)\|\pi_{u}y\|

(10.18)

with an
\epsilon
-slowly varying tempered random variable

\hat{L}_{\epsilon}(\omega)>0

because
\psi_{u}^{-1}(\omega,\cdot)
is tempered
C^{1,\beta_{\alpha}}
on a bounded domain
\{y=\Phi(\omega,x):x\in U_{1}\}
(the boundedness is indicated by (
10.17
)). Then, we conclude that
\psi^{-1}(y)\in U_{\phi}(\omega)
by (
10.16
) and therefore (
10.10
) yields

\displaystyle\|\Phi^{-1}(\omega,y)-\Phi^{-1}(\omega,\pi_{s}y)\|

\displaystyle=\|\phi^{-1}(\omega,\cdot)\circ\psi^{-1}(\omega,y)-\phi^{-1}(\omega,\cdot)\circ\psi_{s}^{-1}(\omega,\pi_{s}y)\|

\displaystyle\leq L_{2}(\omega)\|\psi_{u}^{-1}(\omega,\pi_{u}y)\|^{\hat{\sigma}}\leq L_{3}(\omega)\|\pi_{u}y\|^{\hat{\sigma}}

(10.19)

provided that
\|\pi_{u}y\|<1/\hat{L}_{\epsilon}(\omega)
(implying
\|\psi_{u}^{-1}(\omega,\pi_{u}y)\|<1
by (
10.18
)).

Moreover, for any
y=\Phi(\omega,x)
with
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we also have that

\displaystyle\pi_{u}\Phi^{-1}(\omega,\pi_{s}y)=\pi_{u}\phi^{-1}(\omega,\cdot)\circ\psi^{-1}_{s}(\omega,\pi_{s}y)=0

(10.20)

by (
10
) with
z:=\psi^{-1}_{s}(\omega,\pi_{s}y)\in X_{s}
,

\displaystyle\pi_{u}\Phi^{-1}(\omega,y)=\psi_{u}^{-1}(\omega,\pi_{u}y)\quad{\rm if}~\|\psi_{u}^{-1}(\omega,\pi_{u}y)\|>1/2

(10.21)

by (
10
) with
z:=\psi^{-1}(\omega,y)
, and that

\displaystyle\Phi(\theta\omega,\cdot)\circ F(\omega,\cdot)\circ\Phi^{-1}(\omega,\cdot)=\Lambda(\omega)

(10.22)

by (
10.5
) and (
10.15
).

Having the above preparations, we give the
following lemma.

Lemma 10.2
.

For
U_{1}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|x\|\leq 1/2\}
and for any point
x\in U_{1}\backslash X_{s}
, there is a real
N(\omega,x)>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\pi_{u}F(n,\omega,x)\|>1/2,\quad\forall n\geq N(\omega,x),~\forall\omega\in\Omega,

(10.23)

where
N(\omega,x)
is measurable with respect to
\omega
.

Proof of Lemma
10.2
.
Since
\psi_{u}(\omega,\cdot)
is tempered
C^{1,\beta_{\alpha}}
, we have

\|\psi_{u}(\omega,x_{u})\|\leq\hat{L}_{\epsilon}(\omega)\|x_{u}\|\leq(1/2)\hat{L}_{\epsilon}(\omega),\quad{\rm if}~\|x_{u}\|\leq 1/2,

with an
\epsilon
-slowly varying tempered random variable
\hat{L}_{\epsilon}(\omega)>0
.
It means that, for any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\|\psi_{u}^{-1}(\omega,\pi_{u}y)\|>1/2

if

\|\pi_{u}y\|>\hat{L}_{\epsilon}(\omega)

and therefore (
10.21
) gives

\displaystyle\|\pi_{u}\Phi^{-1}(\omega,y)\|=\|\psi_{u}^{-1}(\omega,\pi_{u}y)\|>1/2\quad{\rm if}~\|\pi_{u}y\|>\hat{L}_{\epsilon}(\omega).

(10.24)

By (
10
) and (
10.20
), we also get

\|\pi_{u}\Phi^{-1}(\omega,y)\|=\|\pi_{u}\Phi^{-1}(\omega,y)-\pi_{u}\Phi^{-1}(\omega,\pi_{s}y)\|\leq L_{3}(\omega)\|\pi_{u}y\|^{\hat{\sigma}}

and therefore

\displaystyle\|\pi_{u}y\|\geq(1/L_{3}(\omega))^{1/\hat{\sigma}}\|\pi_{u}\Phi^{-1}(\omega,y)\|^{1/\hat{\sigma}}\quad{\rm if}~\|\pi_{u}y\|<1/\hat{L}_{\epsilon}(\omega).

(10.25)

Moreover, (
10.12
) implies that

\displaystyle\|\Lambda_{u}(n,\omega)\pi_{u}y\|>e^{\epsilon n}\hat{L}_{\epsilon}(\omega)\geq\hat{L}_{\epsilon}(\theta^{n}\omega),\quad\forall n\geq\frac{1}{\lambda_{\tau}-3\epsilon}\ln\frac{K_{\epsilon}(\omega)\hat{L}_{\epsilon}(\omega)}{\|\pi_{u}y\|}>0.

(10.26)

Next, for any
x\in U_{1}\backslash X_{s}
, set
y:=\Phi(\omega,x)
and consider the case that
\|\pi_{u}y\|<1/\hat{L}_{\epsilon}(\omega)
,
where

\|\pi_{u}y\|\geq(1/L_{3}(\omega))^{1/\hat{\sigma}}\|\pi_{u}x\|^{1/\hat{\sigma}}>0

by (
10.25
). This enables us to choose

N(\omega,x):=\frac{1}{\lambda_{\tau}-3\epsilon}\ln\frac{K_{\epsilon}(\omega)\hat{L}_{\epsilon}(\omega)L_{3}(\omega)^{1/\hat{\sigma}}}{\|\pi_{u}x\|^{1/\hat{\sigma}}}\geq\frac{1}{\lambda_{\tau}-3\epsilon}\ln\frac{K_{\epsilon}(\omega)\hat{L}_{\epsilon}(\omega)}{\|\pi_{u}y\|}>0,

which is clearly measurable with respect to
\omega
.
Then, we
obtain from (
10.22
), (
10.24
) and (
10.26
) that

\displaystyle\|\pi_{u}F(n,\omega,x)\|=\|\pi_{u}F(n,\omega,\cdot)\circ\Phi^{-1}(\omega,y)\|=\|\pi_{u}\Phi^{-1}(\theta^{n}\omega,\cdot)\circ\Lambda(n,\omega)y\|>1/2

for all
n\geq N(\omega,x)
,
which proves (
10.23
) in the case of
\|\pi_{u}y\|<1/\hat{L}_{\epsilon}(\omega)
. In another case that
\|\pi_{u}y\|\geq 1/\hat{L}_{\epsilon}(\omega)
, we choose

N(\omega,x):=\frac{1}{\lambda_{\tau}-3\epsilon}\ln\{K_{\epsilon}(\omega)\hat{L}_{\epsilon}(\omega)^{2}\}\geq\frac{1}{\lambda_{\tau}-3\epsilon}\ln\frac{K_{\epsilon}(\omega)\hat{L}_{\epsilon}(\omega)}{\|\pi_{u}y\|}>0,

which is clearly measurable with respect to
\omega
, we still prove (
10.23
). This completes the proof of Lemma
10.2
.
\Box

We continue to prove Theorem
2.1
.
Fix an arbitrary point
\bar{x}\in U_{1}\backslash{X_{s}}
and let
N(\omega,\bar{x})>0
be given in Lemma
10.2
. Then, we make a decomposition
\Omega=\cup_{m=1}^{\infty}\Omega_{m}
with

\displaystyle\Omega_{m}:=\{\omega\in\Omega:N(\omega,\bar{x})\in(m-1,m]\},\quad\forall m\in\mathbb{N}.

(10.27)

Clearly, every
\Omega_{m}
is measurable since
N(\omega,\bar{x})
is measurable with respect to
\omega
and
(m-1,m]
is a Borel set in
\mathbb{R}
.
By Lemma
10.2
and (
10.27
),
there is a small neighborhood
U(\omega_{m},\bar{x})\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
of
\bar{x}
(not necessarily a tempered ball here) such that

\displaystyle\|\pi_{u}F(m,\omega_{m},x)\|>1/2,\quad\forall x\in U(\omega_{m},\bar{x}),~\forall\omega_{m}\in\Omega_{m}.

This fact together with (
10.13
) implies that

\pi_{u}q_{0}(\theta^{m}\omega_{m},F(m,\omega_{m},x),y_{s})=0

for all
y_{s}\in X_{s}
,
and therefore (
10.1
) tells that the leaf

\mathcal{W}_{s}(\theta^{m}\omega_{m},F(m,\omega_{m},x))

is the graph of the mapping

y_{s}\mapsto\pi_{u}F(m,\omega_{m},x).

Combining this fact with (
4.1
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{s}(\omega_{m},x)

\displaystyle=F(-m,\theta^{m}\omega_{m},\mathcal{W}_{s}(\theta^{m}\omega_{m},F(m,\omega_{m},x)))

\displaystyle=\{F(-m,\theta^{m}\omega_{m},y_{s}+\pi_{u}F(m,\omega_{m},x)):y_{s}\in X_{s}\},\quad\forall x\in U(\omega_{m},\bar{x}).

(10.28)

Then, comparing (
10
) with (
10.1
) (replacing
y_{s}
by
\pi_{s}F(-m,\theta^{m}\omega_{m},y_{s}+\pi_{u}F(m,\omega_{m},x))
), we have that

\pi_{u}F(-m,\theta^{m}\omega_{m},y_{s}+\pi_{u}F(m,\omega_{m},x))=\pi_{u}\{x+q_{0}(\omega_{m},x,\cdot)\circ\pi_{s}F(-m,\theta^{m}\omega_{m},y_{s}+\pi_{u}F(m,\omega_{m},x))\}.

Setting
y_{s}=0
in the above equality, we get by (
10.2
) that

\displaystyle\pi_{u}q_{0}(\omega_{m},x,0)=\pi_{u}F(-m,\theta^{m}\omega_{m},\pi_{u}F(m,\omega_{m},x))-\pi_{u}x,\quad\forall x\in U(\omega_{m},\bar{x}),

(10.29)

whose right-hand side clearly has a derivative measurable with respect to
\omega_{m}
by our assumption that
F(\omega,\cdot)
is a
C^{2,\alpha}
random diffeomorphism, i.e.,
F^{\pm 1}(\omega,\cdot)
together with their derivatives are measurable with respect to
\omega
. Hence,
\partial_{x}(\pi_{u}q_{0})(\omega_{m},x,0)
is measurable with respect to
\omega_{m}\in\Omega_{m}
. Since
\Omega
is a union of countably many measurable sets
\Omega_{m}
, we further conclude that
\partial_{x}(\pi_{u}q_{0})(\omega,x,0)
is measurable with respect to
\omega\in\Omega
for
x\in U(\omega,\bar{x})
. Namely, in the case of
\bar{x}\in U_{1}\backslash{X_{s}}
, we have that

(C)

\partial_{x}(\pi_{u}q_{0})(\omega,\bar{x},0)
is measurable with respect to
\omega\in\Omega
.

Next, in the case of
\bar{x}\in{X_{s}}\cap U_{1}
, we find a sequence
\{\bar{x}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
U_{1}\backslash{X_{s}}
such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\bar{x}_{n}=\bar{x}.

Since
q_{0}(\omega,\cdot,0)
is continuously differentiable (actually
C^{1,\beta_{\alpha}}
) in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, as mentioned at the beginning of this proof, we get

\lim_{n\to\infty}\partial_{x}(\pi_{u}q_{0})(\omega,\bar{x}_{n},0)=\partial_{x}(\pi_{u}q_{0})(\omega,\bar{x},0),\quad\forall\omega\in\Omega,

which together with (C) implies that
\partial_{x}(\pi_{u}q_{0})(\omega,x,0)
is measurable with respect to
\omega
for
x\in U_{1}
. Similarly, we also prove that

\partial_{x}(\pi_{s}p_{0})(\omega,x,0)
is measurable with respect to
\omega
for
x\in U_{1}
.

Hence,
D\phi(\omega,x)
is measurable with respect to
\omega\in\Omega
by (
10.3
). Moreover, noting that
\phi^{-1}(\omega,x)
is a random mapping (see Lemma
10.1
) and that

D\phi^{-1}(\omega,x)=\{D\phi(\omega,\phi^{-1}(\omega,x))\}^{-1},

we obtain that
D\phi^{-1}(\omega,x)
is measurable with respect to
\omega
.
It follows that the mapping
\Phi(\omega,\cdot)
defined in (
10.16
) is a
C^{1,\beta_{\alpha}}
random diffeomorphism in
U_{1}\supset U(\omega)
, which proves (ii) of Theorem
2.1
. Since

\Phi(\omega,\cdot)
straightens up the stable and unstable foliations by (
10.16
), we also prove
(i) of Theorem
2.1
and
the proof is completed.
\Box

Appendix

In this appendix,
we prove (
3
).
Putting
\hat{\rho}(\omega):=\rho(\omega)^{2}>0
, we define

\displaystyle g(\omega,s):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}e^{\,\hat{\rho}(\omega)^{2}(s-\hat{\rho}(\omega)/2)^{-1}(s-\hat{\rho}(\omega))^{-1}},&\hat{\rho}(\omega)/2<s<\hat{\rho}(\omega),\\
0,&\mbox{other cases},\end{array}\right.

and

\displaystyle{h}(\omega,s):=\frac{\int_{s}^{+\infty}g(\omega,\varsigma)d\varsigma}{\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}g(\omega,\varsigma)d\varsigma}.

It is clear that
h(\omega,\cdot):\mathbb{R}\to(0,1)
is
smooth such that
h(\omega,s)=1
for all
s\leq\hat{\rho}(\omega)/2
and
h(\omega,s)=0
for all
s\geq\hat{\rho}(\omega)
. For the estimate of its derivative,
we have that the maximum value of
g(\omega,s)
is
e^{-16}
for all
\omega
and
s
. Computing the points at which the values of
g(\omega,\cdot)
are
e^{-16}/2
, i.e.,

\frac{3}{4}\hat{\rho}(\omega)\pm\bigg(\frac{1}{16}-\frac{1}{\ln 2+16}\bigg)^{1/2}\hat{\rho}(\omega)\in(\hat{\rho}(\omega)/2,\hat{\rho}(\omega)),

we have the estimates

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\hat{\rho}(\omega)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}g(\omega,\varsigma)d\varsigma\geq\bigg(\frac{1}{16}-\frac{1}{\ln 2+16}\bigg)^{1/2}e^{-16},

and therefore

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(\omega)|Dh(\omega,s)|\leq\frac{\hat{\rho}(\omega)|g(\omega,s)|}{\int_{0}^{+\infty}g(\omega,\varsigma)d\varsigma}\leq\bigg(\frac{1}{16}-\frac{1}{\ln 2+16}\bigg)^{-1/2}.

(A.1)

For the second order derivative of
h(\omega,\cdot)
, noting that

\max_{a\geq 0}\{e^{-a}a^{r}\}\leq e^{-r}r^{r}

for
r\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(\omega)|Dg(\omega,s)|

\displaystyle\leq e^{\,\hat{\rho}(\omega)^{2}(s-\hat{\rho}(\omega)/2)^{-1}(s-\hat{\rho}(\omega))^{-1}}\frac{\hat{\rho}(\omega)^{3}|2s-3\hat{\rho}(\omega)/2|}{(s-\hat{\rho}(\omega)/2)^{2}(s-\hat{\rho}(\omega))^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\{e^{\,\hat{\rho}(\omega)^{2}(s-\hat{\rho}(\omega)/2)^{-1}(s-\hat{\rho}(\omega))^{-1}}\frac{\hat{\rho}(\omega)^{4}}{(s-\hat{\rho}(\omega)/2)^{2}(s-\hat{\rho}(\omega))^{2}}\bigg\}\frac{|2s-3\hat{\rho}(\omega)/2|}{\hat{\rho}(\omega)}

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{-2}

for
s\in(\hat{\rho}(\omega)/2,\,\hat{\rho}(\omega))
, and
therefore
\hat{\rho}(\omega)|Dg(\omega,s)|\leq 2e^{-2}
for all

s\in\mathbb{R}
since
g(\omega,s)=0
outside
(\hat{\rho}(\omega)/2,\hat{\rho}(\omega))
. Thus,

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(\omega)^{2}|D^{2}{h}(\omega,s)|\leq\frac{\hat{\rho}(\omega)^{2}|Dg(\omega,s)|}{\int_{0}^{+\infty}g(\omega,\varsigma)d\varsigma}\leq 2\bigg(\frac{1}{16}-\frac{1}{\ln 2+16}\bigg)^{-1/2}e^{14}.

(A.2)

Similarly, for the third order derivative we have the estimate

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(\omega)^{3}|D^{3}{h}(\omega,s)|\leq(4e^{-4}+(9/2)e^{-3}+8e^{-2})\bigg(\frac{1}{16}-\frac{1}{\ln 2+16}\bigg)^{-1/2}e^{16}

(A.3)

for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

Next, let
U(\omega)
and
V(\omega)
be neighborhoods of the origin with diameters
\rho(\omega)/2
and
\rho(\omega)
respectively, and define
u(\omega,\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to(0,1)
as

\displaystyle u(\omega,x):=h\Big(\omega,\sum_{j=1,...,{p}}\,\sum_{\iota=1,...,d_{j}}(\pi_{j,\iota}x)^{2}\Big).

Then we verify that

\displaystyle u(\omega,x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}1,&\forall x\in U(\omega),\\
0,&\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash V(\omega).\end{array}\right.

Moreover, (
A.1
)-(
A.3
) give

\displaystyle\rho^{r}(\omega)\|D^{r}u(\omega,x)\|\leq 2r\rho^{2r}(\omega)\Big|D^{r}h\Big(\sum_{j=1,...,{p}}\,\sum_{\iota=1,...,d_{j}}(\pi_{j,\iota}x)^{2}\Big)\Big|\leq C_{u},~~~r=1,2,3,

where

C_{u}:=6(4e^{-4}+(9/2)e^{-3}+8e^{-2})\bigg(\frac{1}{16}-\frac{1}{\ln 2+16}\bigg)^{-1/2}e^{16}>0.

This proves (
3
).
